## [1284] Paracetamol toxicity: NAC protocol, King's College criteria for transplant

**Cluster**: paracetamol | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Paracetamol poisoning is treated with prompt acetylcysteine, which prevents liver injury when started early. Two-bag regimens are effective with fewer anaphylactoid reactions, and consensus statements standardise assessment and triage.

### Core Teaching Points
- A US and Canada consensus statement gives evidence-based recommendations for management of acetaminophen poisoning, and a separate consensus guides out-of-hospital triage of paracetamol exposure. (PMID 37552484, PMID 40047505)
- Early N-acetylcysteine, with treatment from 8 to 24 hours after overdose, is important to prevent or minimise liver damage regardless of regimen or route. (PMID 36034775)
- A two-bag acetylcysteine regimen was safe and effective and significantly reduced non-allergic anaphylactoid reactions compared with a three-bag regimen (OR 0.24). (PMID 40013897)
- The paracetamol-aminotransferase multiplication product can be used more than 24 hours after overdose and in staggered ingestions, addressing limitations of the Rumack-Matthew nomogram. (PMID 36444937)
- There is insufficient evidence to recommend fomepizole or cimetidine as primary or adjunct therapy in severe paracetamol poisoning. (PMID 32762579)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Treatment of late presentations and staggered overdoses.
- Optimal treatment duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic sequencing in high-risk subgroups of Paracetamol toxicity: NAC protocol, King's College criteria for transplant.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Rumack-Matthew nomogram from 4 hours.
- Acetylcysteine is most effective within 8 hours.
- Diagnostic criteria, biomarker thresholds, and risk stratification in Paracetamol toxicity: NAC protocol, King's College criteria for transplant.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient presenting 6 hours after an intentional paracetamol overdose.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Paracetamol toxicity: NAC protocol, King's College criteria for transplant presenting with therapeutic intolerance, refractory symptoms, or acute complication requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: King's College criteria for transplant, nomogram thresholds and acetylcysteine doses are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32762579**: *Metabolic and mitochondrial treatments for severe paracetamol poisoning: a systematic review* (2020) [systematic review] - None
- **PMID 36034775**: *N-Acetylcysteine for Preventing Acetaminophen-Induced Liver Injury: A Comprehensive Review* (2022) [systematic review] - None
- **PMID 36444937**: *Performance of the paracetamol-aminotransferase multiplication product in risk stratification after paracetamol (acetaminophen) poisoning: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2023) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 37552484**: *Management of Acetaminophen Poisoning in the US and Canada: A Consensus Statement* (2023) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 40013897**: *Comparison of two-bag and three-bag acetylcysteine regimens in the treatment of paracetamol poisoning: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 40047505**: *Out-of-hospital assessment and triage of paracetamol (acetaminophen) exposure in the United States and Canada: a consensus guideline* (2025) [guideline] - None

## [1285] Paraganglioma: extra-adrenal location, SDHB mutation, malignant potential, surveillance

**Cluster**: paraganglioma | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Paragangliomas, especially extra-adrenal tumours with germline SDHB variants, carry a high metastatic risk. Genetic testing guides surveillance and management, and treatment of metastases combines local and systemic options.

### Core Teaching Points
- The international SDHB consensus states that management of metastases in SDHB-related PPGL is challenging and relies on local or systemic therapies, or both. (PMID 38097671)
- Patients with a pathogenic germline variant have a higher rate of metastasis than patients with no identified variant. (PMID 41183483)
- NANETS consensus guidelines address surveillance after resection and management of metastatic or unresectable pheochromocytoma and paraganglioma. (PMID 33939658)
- The Japan Endocrine Society guideline notes genetic testing guides management of different variants and molecularly targeted drugs are advancing. (PMID 41083371)
- European guidance exists for adrenal and extra-adrenal paraganglioma in children and adolescents. (PMID 41261973)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal systemic therapy for metastatic disease.
- Optimal treatment duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic sequencing in high-risk subgroups of Paraganglioma: extra-adrenal location, SDHB mutation, malignant potential, surveillance.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- SDHB mutations and malignant potential.
- Alpha-blockade before surgery for functioning tumours (not in abstracts).
- Diagnostic criteria, biomarker thresholds, and risk stratification in Paraganglioma: extra-adrenal location, SDHB mutation, malignant potential, surveillance.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Young patient with a carotid body paraganglioma and a family history.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Paraganglioma: extra-adrenal location, SDHB mutation, malignant potential, surveillance presenting with therapeutic intolerance, refractory symptoms, or acute complication requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Biochemical screening (plasma metanephrines), imaging choice and perioperative blockade are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33939658**: *The North American Neuroendocrine Tumor Society Consensus Guidelines for Surveillance and Management of Metastatic and/or Unresectable Pheochromocytoma and Paraganglioma* (2021) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 38097671**: *Management of phaeochromocytoma and paraganglioma in patients with germline SDHB pathogenic variants: an international expert Consensus statement* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 41083371**: *Japan Endocrine Society Clinical Practice Guideline for the Diagnosis and Management of Pheochromocytoma and Paraganglioma 2025* (2026) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 41183483**: *Metastatic Disease in Phaeochromocytomas and Paragangliomas in Various Genotypes-A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis* (2026) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 41261973**: *European clinical guidance for the management of adrenal and extra-adrenal paraganglioma in children and adolescents: a consensus by the EXPeRT group* (2025) [guideline] - None

## [1286] Paraneoplastic neurological syndromes: Anti-Hu, Anti-Yo, Anti-Ri

**Cluster**: paraneoplastic | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Paraneoplastic and autoimmune neurological syndromes are diagnosed by antibody testing, CSF, MRI and FDG-PET, with a search for an underlying tumour. Management is multidisciplinary immunotherapy plus tumour treatment.

### Core Teaching Points
- UK national guidelines on suspected infectious and autoimmune encephalitis in adults stress multidisciplinary input to reduce mortality and long-term neurological morbidity. (PMID 42863638)
- A meta-analysis of FDG PET in autoimmune encephalitis found antibody-related patterns of regional metabolic abnormality, supporting its role in diagnosis and prognosis. (PMID 39601059)
- Neurofilament light chain was higher in serum and CSF in neuronal surface antibody-associated encephalitis than in controls but was not significantly related to clinical or paraclinical features. (PMID 39856041)
- Autoimmune encephalitis after herpes simplex encephalitis has age-specific features and improved outcomes with immunotherapy, which was confirmed as safe. (PMID 40780589)
- Seronegative autoimmune encephalitis is diagnostically challenging and needs biomarker discovery and prospective cohorts. (PMID 41499723)
- NCCN guidelines for thymoma and thymic carcinoma highlight multidisciplinary management, relevant as thymoma is a paraneoplastic neurological association. (PMID 40499586)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Predictors of response to immunotherapy in seronegative disease.
- Optimal treatment duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic sequencing in high-risk subgroups of Paraneoplastic neurological syndromes: Anti-Hu, Anti-Yo, Anti-Ri.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Anti-Hu with small cell lung cancer; anti-Yo with ovarian or breast cancer (not in abstracts).
- Search for tumour when onconeural antibodies are positive.
- Diagnostic criteria, biomarker thresholds, and risk stratification in Paraneoplastic neurological syndromes: Anti-Hu, Anti-Yo, Anti-Ri.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Smoker with subacute sensory neuronopathy and anti-Hu antibodies.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Paraneoplastic neurological syndromes: Anti-Hu, Anti-Yo, Anti-Ri presenting with therapeutic intolerance, refractory symptoms, or acute complication requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Antibody-specific tumour associations (Hu, Yo, Ri, NMDAR) and screening imaging strategy are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39601059**: *Regional Metabolic Abnormalities in Autoimmune Encephalitis: A Meta-analysis of 498 Cases With Brain FDG PET* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 39856041**: *Neurofilament light chain levels in neuronal surface antibody-associated autoimmune encephalitis: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 40499586**: *Thymomas and Thymic Carcinomas, Version 2.2025, NCCN Clinical Practice Guidelines In Oncology* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 40780589**: *Clinical phenotype and outcomes in autoimmune encephalitis after herpes simplex virus encephalitis: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 41499723**: *Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of the Clinical Features Associated With Seronegative Autoimmune Encephalitis* (2026) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 42863638**: *Management of suspected infectious and autoimmune encephalitis in adults - National Guidelines from the United Kingdom Joint Societies* (2026) [guideline] - None

## [1287] Parapneumonic Effusion and Empyema

**Cluster**: empyema | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Parapneumonic effusion and empyema are treated with antibiotics and drainage, with intrapleural fibrinolytics or surgery for loculated disease. Paediatric data show chest tube with fibrinolytics is lower in cost with similar outcomes to surgery.

### Core Teaching Points
- A Cochrane review found intrapleural fibrinolytic therapy in adult parapneumonic effusion reduced overall treatment failure (OR 0.16, low-certainty evidence). (PMID 31684683)
- A living systematic review in children found a chest tube plus fibrinolytics costs less, with similar length of stay and clinical outcomes to other management options. (PMID 37463660)
- Implementing a clinical practice guideline for paediatric empyema improved adherence to national guidelines without changing length of stay or treatment failure rates. (PMID 39423731)
- Japanese guidelines classify empyema into acute, chronic and postoperative types and give surgical recommendations for each. (PMID 39969668)
- A 2026 IDSA/PIDS guideline addresses paediatric pneumonia and pleural infection with GRADE methodology. (PMID 41839546)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Best first-line approach in adults (fibrinolysis versus early surgery).
- Optimal management duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic de-escalation in high-risk subgroups of Parapneumonic Effusion and Empyema.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Pleural fluid pH below 7.2 or pus indicates drainage.
- Intrapleural tPA with DNase (not in abstracts).
- Recognizing treatment refractoriness, adverse drug reaction monitoring, and urgent escalation pathways in Parapneumonic Effusion and Empyema.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Pneumonia not responding to antibiotics with a loculated effusion on ultrasound.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Parapneumonic Effusion and Empyema presenting with therapeutic intolerance, contraindications, or disease escalation requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Light criteria, pleural fluid pH and glucose thresholds, MIST data and antibiotic duration are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31684683**: *Intra-pleural fibrinolytic therapy versus placebo, or a different fibrinolytic agent, in the treatment of adult parapneumonic effusions and empyema* (2019) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 37463660**: *Conservative and Surgical Modalities in the Management of Pediatric Parapneumonic Effusion and Empyema: A Living Systematic Review and Network Meta-Analysis* (2023) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 39423731**: *Implications of Using a Clinical Practice Guideline on Outcomes in Pediatric Empyema* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 39969668**: *Guidelines for the treatment of empyema (The Japanese Association for Chest Surgery)* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 41839546**: *Clinical Practice Guideline by the Infectious Diseases Society of America and the Pediatric Infectious Diseases Society: 2026 Guideline Update on The Management of Community-Acquired Pneumonia in Infants and Children Older than 3 Months of Age* (2026) [guideline] - None

## [1288] Parenteral Nutrition

**Cluster**: parenteral_nutrition | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Parenteral nutrition is reserved for patients who cannot be fed enterally, with ESPEN practical guidance for home PN, micronutrients and lipid emulsions. In critical illness early enteral feeding is preferred where the gut works.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ESPEN practical guideline on home parenteral nutrition provides recommendations using SIGN methodology. (PMID 36796121)
- The ESPEN short micronutrient guideline offers practical advice on provision and monitoring of micronutrients during nutritional support in at-risk diseases. (PMID 38350290)
- Expert consensus statements from the Lipid Summit address intravenous lipid emulsions in long-term and home parenteral nutrition. (PMID 41438196)
- In critically ill patients with a functioning gut, early enteral nutrition is favoured over early parenteral nutrition for infection control and recovery. (PMID 39796444)
- A GI diseases nutrition guideline recommends nutritional support within 72 hours of admission when oral intake covers under 60% of protein-calorie needs. (PMID 40035369)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Lipid emulsion choice.
- Optimal management duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic de-escalation in high-risk subgroups of Parenteral Nutrition.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Complications: line sepsis, refeeding, liver disease.
- Enteral nutrition preferred when the gut works.
- Recognizing treatment refractoriness, adverse drug reaction monitoring, and urgent escalation pathways in Parenteral Nutrition.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient with short bowel syndrome on home parenteral nutrition with raised liver enzymes.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Parenteral Nutrition presenting with therapeutic intolerance, contraindications, or disease escalation requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Indications for PN, access, macronutrient prescription and complications monitoring are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36796121**: *ESPEN practical guideline: Home parenteral nutrition* (2023) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 38350290**: *ESPEN practical short micronutrient guideline* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 39796444**: *Comparison of Early Enteral Nutrition Versus Early Parenteral Nutrition in Critically Ill Patients: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 40035369**: *Clinical nutrition in gastrointestinal diseases: an up-to-date clinical practice guideline* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 41438196**: *Long-term/home parenteral nutrition: expert consensus statements regarding intravenous lipid emulsions* (2025) [guideline] - None

## [1289] parkinson&#039;s disease levodopa

**Cluster**: parkinson | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Parkinson disease care covers dopaminergic therapy, management of motor complications, and referral for deep brain stimulation or other advanced therapies. Retrieval gave guideline material on non-motor problems and referral, not on levodopa itself.

### Core Teaching Points
- A consensus on referral for deep brain stimulation provides a framework so that appropriate candidates have timely access to surgery and misconceptions about brain surgery are addressed. (PMID 41484162)
- A Gulf consensus uses the 5-2-1 criteria for advanced therapy referral and proposes a tiered network model for Parkinson disease care. (PMID 42656222)
- The German guideline gives a practice-oriented stepwise approach to diagnosis and treatment of autonomic failure, pain and sleep disturbances in Parkinson disease, and another section covers impulse control disorders, psychosis and delirium. (PMID 39751950, PMID 39046524)
- Best practice guidelines address diagnosis, evaluation and management of cognitive disorders in Parkinson disease to optimise cognitive care. (PMID 41870152)
- A Gulf consensus covers safe Ramadan fasting in Parkinson disease with shared decision-making on motor and non-motor management. (PMID 41458120)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Levodopa dosing strategy and management of motor fluctuations are not covered by the retrieved abstracts.
- Optimal management duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic de-escalation in high-risk subgroups of parkinson&#039;s disease levodopa.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Referral for DBS when motor complications persist despite optimised medication.
- Psychosis in Parkinson disease: avoid typical antipsychotics (not in abstracts).
- Recognizing treatment refractoriness, adverse drug reaction monitoring, and urgent escalation pathways in parkinson&#039;s disease levodopa.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient on levodopa 6 years with wearing-off and dyskinesia.
- A complex multimorbid patient with parkinson&#039;s disease levodopa presenting with therapeutic intolerance, contraindications, or disease escalation requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Levodopa and dopamine agonist therapy, COMT/MAO-B inhibitors, motor complication management and DBS outcomes are not stated in the retrieved abstracts; needs a core Parkinson guideline (NICE NG71 or MDS) before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39046524**: *Diagnostics and treatment of impulse control disorders, psychosis and delirium: systemic review-based recommendations - guideline "Parkinson's disease" of the German Society of Neurology* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 39751950**: *Diagnosis and treatment of autonomic failure, pain and sleep disturbances in Parkinson's disease: guideline "Parkinson's disease" of the German Society of Neurology* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 41458120**: *Faith, fasting, and well-being: Emirates Neurology Society consensus guidelines on safe Ramadan fasting in Parkinson's disease* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 41484162**: *Consensus expert recommendations for referral of Parkinson's disease patients for deep brain stimulation surgery* (2026) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 41870152**: *Best practice guidelines for the diagnosis, evaluation, and management of cognitive disorders in Parkinson's disease* (2026) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 42656222**: *Parkinson's disease in the United Arab Emirates (UAE): Emirates Neurology Society consensus guidelines* (2026) [guideline] - None

- A Gulf consensus covers safe Ramadan fasting in Parkinson disease with shared decision-making on motor and non-motor management. (PMID 41458120)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Levodopa dosing strategy and management of motor fluctuations are not covered by the retrieved abstracts.
- Optimal therapeutic timing for initiating device-aided therapies (subcutaneous apomorphine, levodopa intestinal gel, DBS) before irreversible motor decline.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Referral for DBS when motor complications persist despite optimised medication.
- Psychosis in Parkinson disease: avoid typical antipsychotics (not in abstracts).
- Differentiating idiopathic Parkinson disease from atypical parkinsonian syndromes (PSP, MSA, DLB) based on early falls, autonomic failure, and symmetrical poor levodopa response.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient on levodopa 6 years with wearing-off and dyskinesia.
- 70-year-old male with a 2-year history of resting tremor develops vivid visual hallucinations and orthostatic syncope shortly after escalating ropinirole.

**Reviewer Gap**: Levodopa and dopamine agonist therapy, COMT/MAO-B inhibitors, motor complication management and DBS outcomes are not stated in the retrieved abstracts; needs a core Parkinson guideline (NICE NG71 or MDS) before approval.

## [1294] Parkinson's disease

**Cluster**: parkinson | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Parkinson disease care covers dopaminergic therapy, management of motor complications, and referral for deep brain stimulation or other advanced therapies. Retrieval gave guideline material on non-motor problems and referral, not on levodopa itself.

### Core Teaching Points
- A consensus on referral for deep brain stimulation provides a framework so that appropriate candidates have timely access to surgery and misconceptions about brain surgery are addressed. (PMID 41484162)
- A Gulf consensus uses the 5-2-1 criteria for advanced therapy referral and proposes a tiered network model for Parkinson disease care. (PMID 42656222)
- The German guideline gives a practice-oriented stepwise approach to diagnosis and treatment of autonomic failure, pain and sleep disturbances in Parkinson disease, and another section covers impulse control disorders, psychosis and delirium. (PMID 39751950, PMID 39046524)
- Best practice guidelines address diagnosis, evaluation and management of cognitive disorders in Parkinson disease to optimise cognitive care. (PMID 41870152)
- A Gulf consensus covers safe Ramadan fasting in Parkinson disease with shared decision-making on motor and non-motor management. (PMID 41458120)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Levodopa dosing strategy and management of motor fluctuations are not covered by the retrieved abstracts.
- Optimal treatment duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic sequencing in high-risk subgroups of Parkinson's disease.

- Referral for DBS when motor complications persist despite optimised medication.

## [1295] Paroxysmal Nocturnal Haemoglobinuria

**Cluster**: pnh | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Paroxysmal nocturnal haemoglobinuria is diagnosed by flow cytometric clone detection and treated with terminal or proximal complement inhibitors. Breakthrough haemolysis and residual anaemia are the main management challenges.

### Core Teaching Points
- An expert consensus addresses management of pharmacodynamic breakthrough haemolysis in treated PNH, based on clinical experience in the absence of randomised data. (PMID 39665683)
- Expert consensus covers monitoring and treatment of PNH clones in patients with aplastic anaemia in Asia, including when haemolysis is mild or moderate. (PMID 39596227)
- A guideline update notes the principal goal is control of haemolysis and that approaches to residual anaemia and fatigue need refinement as new complement-targeting drugs emerge. (PMID 39505557)
- Chinese and Singapore consensus documents give recommendations for screening, diagnosis and management, and a Chinese consensus covers clonal screening and monitoring of complement inhibitor therapy. (PMID 39307719, PMID 38979993, PMID 38604785)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Choice between C5 and proximal complement inhibitors.
- Optimal management duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic de-escalation in high-risk subgroups of Paroxysmal Nocturnal Haemoglobinuria.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Flow cytometry for GPI-anchored proteins (CD55/CD59).
- Thrombosis as the main cause of death (not in abstracts).
- Recognizing treatment refractoriness, adverse drug reaction monitoring, and urgent escalation pathways in Paroxysmal Nocturnal Haemoglobinuria.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Young adult with dark morning urine, haemolytic anaemia and Budd-Chiari syndrome.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Paroxysmal Nocturnal Haemoglobinuria presenting with therapeutic intolerance, contraindications, or disease escalation requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Clinical triad, thrombosis prophylaxis and eculizumab/ravulizumab specifics are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources

## [1296] Patent foramen ovale closure after cryptogenic stroke

**Cluster**: pfo | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
PFO closure is considered in selected younger patients with cryptogenic stroke after careful work-up; residual shunt after closure raises recurrence risk, and evidence in patients over 60 is limited.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ESO guidelines give recommendations and consensus statements on diagnostic methods for PFO detection and on medical management after PFO closure. (PMID 38752755)
- A meta-analysis found a residual shunt after PFO closure significantly increased the risk of recurrent cerebrovascular events. (PMID 36882039)
- In patients over 60 with cryptogenic stroke, evidence for PFO closure is limited by confounding and short follow-up, and long-term randomised trials are essential. (PMID 41099439)
- British guidance on agitated saline contrast echocardiography describes when to move to advanced imaging and uses frameworks such as the RoPE score. (PMID 42219525)
- In acute pulmonary embolism, PFO was associated with higher ischaemic stroke incidence (OR 5.36) and increased mortality. (PMID 39622459)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Selection of older patients for closure.
- Optimal management duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic de-escalation in high-risk subgroups of Patent foramen ovale closure after cryptogenic stroke.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- RoPE score to estimate attribution of stroke to PFO.
- Antithrombotic therapy after closure.
- Recognizing treatment refractoriness, adverse drug reaction monitoring, and urgent escalation pathways in Patent foramen ovale closure after cryptogenic stroke.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 38-year-old with an embolic-pattern infarct and a large right-to-left shunt on bubble study.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Patent foramen ovale closure after cryptogenic stroke presenting with therapeutic intolerance, contraindications, or disease escalation requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Trial results (CLOSE, RESPECT, REDUCE), large shunt and atrial septal aneurysm criteria are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36882039**: *A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of the Association between Residual Shunts after Patent Foramen Ovale Closure and Long-Term Cerebrovascular Events* (2023) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 38752755**: *European Stroke Organisation (ESO) Guidelines on the diagnosis and management of patent foramen ovale (PFO) after stroke* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 39622459**: *Risk of ischemic stroke in patients with pulmonary embolism and patent foramen ovale: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None

## [1297] Patient-ventilator dyssynchrony: recognition and management

**Cluster**: ventilator_dyssynchrony | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Patient-ventilator asynchrony, particularly ineffective and double triggering, is associated with longer mechanical ventilation and ICU stay. Proportional modes such as NAVA improve synchrony, with some evidence of weaning benefit.

### Core Teaching Points
- A meta-analysis found patient-ventilator asynchrony, specifically ineffective triggering and double triggering, was associated with longer mechanical ventilation and ICU length of stay. (PMID 40793855)
- Neurally adjusted ventilatory assist improves patient-ventilator interaction, but effects on patient-important outcomes remain uncertain. (PMID 30882481)
- Compared with other partial support modes, NAVA may improve weaning success in difficult-to-wean adults, and a later meta-analysis suggested improved synchronisation and outcomes in acute respiratory failure compared with conventional modes. (PMID 34187528, PMID 35273975)
- A review addresses monitoring of asynchrony during non-invasive ventilation, and machine learning detection of asynchrony is developing but needs standardised definitions. (PMID 36743668, PMID 40640842)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether correcting asynchrony improves survival.
- Optimal treatment duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic sequencing in high-risk subgroups of Patient-ventilator dyssynchrony: recognition and management.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Ineffective triggering and auto-PEEP.
- Double triggering and short inspiratory time.
- Diagnostic criteria, biomarker thresholds, and risk stratification in Patient-ventilator dyssynchrony: recognition and management.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Ventilated COPD patient with ineffective efforts on the flow-time waveform.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Patient-ventilator dyssynchrony: recognition and management presenting with therapeutic intolerance, refractory symptoms, or acute complication requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Waveform recognition of each asynchrony type and adjustment of trigger, cycling and sedation are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30882481**: *Effect of Neurally Adjusted Ventilatory Assist on Patient-Ventilator Interaction in Mechanically Ventilated Adults: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2019) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 34187528**: *Neurally adjusted ventilatory assist as a weaning mode for adults with invasive mechanical ventilation: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2021) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 35273975**: *Neurally Adjusted Ventilatory Assist vs. Conventional Mechanical Ventilation in Adults and Children With Acute Respiratory Failure: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2022) [meta-analysis] - None

## [1298] PCSK9 inhibitor evolocumab cardiovascular outcomes

**Cluster**: pcsk9 | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Evolocumab lowered LDL cholesterol by about 60% on top of statins and reduced cardiovascular events in FOURIER. Benefit is greatest in high-risk patients, and PCSK9 inhibitors or ezetimibe add little in low-to-moderate risk groups.

### Core Teaching Points
- FOURIER: evolocumab on a statin background lowered LDL cholesterol (59% reduction versus placebo, median 30 mg/dL) and reduced cardiovascular events, showing patients with atherosclerotic disease benefit from lowering LDL below current targets. (PMID 28304224)
- A BMJ network meta-analysis found that among moderate- and low-risk groups, adding a PCSK9 inhibitor or ezetimibe to statins gave little or no benefit for MI and stroke. (PMID 35508321)
- A meta-analysis of 35 randomised trials found PCSK9 inhibitors improve cardiovascular outcomes without overall benefit on all-cause or cardiovascular mortality, a benefit that may be reachable with higher baseline LDL. (PMID 29223954)
- Lowering LDL cholesterol is as effective at reducing vascular events in older patients as in those under 75, supporting non-statin therapy in older patients. (PMID 33186535)
- PCSK9 inhibition significantly reduced the risk of venous thromboembolism, with lipoprotein(a) reduction a possible mediator. (PMID 32223429)
- The 2018 AHA/ACC systematic review found benefit for ezetimibe and PCSK9 inhibitors added to statins, but not for niacin or CETP inhibitors. (PMID 30586775)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Mortality benefit in all-cause outcomes.
- Optimal management duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic de-escalation in high-risk subgroups of PCSK9 inhibitor evolocumab cardiovascular outcomes.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Who qualifies for PCSK9 inhibitors: very high risk above LDL threshold on maximal statin and ezetimibe (not in abstracts).
- Injection frequency and cost.
- Recognizing treatment refractoriness, adverse drug reaction monitoring, and urgent escalation pathways in PCSK9 inhibitor evolocumab cardiovascular outcomes.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Post-MI patient with LDL-C 2.2 mmol/L on high-intensity statin plus ezetimibe.
- A complex multimorbid patient with PCSK9 inhibitor evolocumab cardiovascular outcomes presenting with therapeutic intolerance, contraindications, or disease escalation requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Eligibility thresholds and cost-effectiveness rules differ by country and are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28304224**: *Evolocumab and Clinical Outcomes in Patients with Cardiovascular Disease* (2017) [other] - None
- **PMID 29223954**: *Effect of PCSK9 Inhibitors on Clinical Outcomes in Patients With Hypercholesterolemia: A Meta-Analysis of 35 Randomized Controlled Trials* (2017) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 30586775**: *Systematic Review for the 2018 AHA/ACC/AACVPR/AAPA/ABC/ACPM/ADA/AGS/APhA/ASPC/NLA/PCNA Guideline on the Management of Blood Cholesterol: A Report of the American College of Cardiology/American Heart Association Task Force on Clinical Practice Guidelines* (2019) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 32223429**: *The Effect of PCSK9 (Proprotein Convertase Subtilisin/Kexin Type 9) Inhibition on the Risk of Venous Thromboembolism* (2020) [meta-analysis] - None

- **PMID 35508321**: *PCSK9 inhibitors and ezetimibe with or without statin therapy for cardiovascular risk reduction: a systematic review and network meta-analysis* (2022) [meta-analysis] - None

## [1299] PEA and asystole: reversible causes, ACLS algorithm, high-quality CPR

**Cluster**: cardiac_arrest_rhythms | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Non-shockable arrest rhythms (PEA and asystole) are managed with high-quality CPR, early adrenaline and a search for reversible causes. The 2025 ERC and AHA guidelines emphasise early effective ALS and distinguishing causative from secondary arrhythmias.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ERC 2025 adult ALS guidelines emphasise providing early and effective ALS interventions to improve survival from in- and out-of-hospital cardiac arrest. (PMID 41117572)
- The 2025 AHA adult ALS guideline underscores identifying causative versus secondary arrhythmias to inform timing of therapeutic strategies. (PMID 41122884)
- ERC and AHA special circumstances guidelines describe modifications to basic and advanced life support for conditions that cause arrest, with separate graded recommendations for adults and children. (PMID 41117569, PMID 41122889)
- The 2025 AHA post-cardiac arrest care guideline covers care after return of spontaneous circulation, survivorship and organ donation. (PMID 41122894)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Evidence for individual drugs and airway strategies is covered in the full guidelines.
- Optimal management duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic de-escalation in high-risk subgroups of PEA and asystole: reversible causes, ACLS algorithm, high-quality CPR.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Reversible causes (4 Hs and 4 Ts) in PEA.
- Asystole: check leads, confirm rhythm, adrenaline every 3-5 minutes (not in abstracts).
- Recognizing treatment refractoriness, adverse drug reaction monitoring, and urgent escalation pathways in PEA and asystole: reversible causes, ACLS algorithm, high-quality CPR.

### Case Generation Hooks
- In-hospital arrest with PEA and a history of massive haemoptysis.
- A complex multimorbid patient with PEA and asystole: reversible causes, ACLS algorithm, high-quality CPR presenting with therapeutic intolerance, contraindications, or disease escalation requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: The algorithm (compressions, adrenaline timing, rhythm checks) and the list of reversible causes are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 41117572**: *European Resuscitation Council Guidelines 2025 Adult Advanced Life Support* (2025) [guideline] - None

## [1300] Peanut oral immunotherapy food allergy

**Cluster**: peanut_oit | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Oral immunotherapy can desensitise people with peanut and other IgE-mediated food allergies, with omalizumab adjunct therapy improving safety. Long-term outcomes, optimal duration and cost-effectiveness remain open.

### Core Teaching Points
- The EAACI 2025 guidelines on IgE-mediated food allergy note future research is needed on disease-modifying treatments with standardised patient-focused outcomes. (PMID 39473345)
- Omalizumab with oral immunotherapy in children enhances the safety of OIT, but optimal duration, long-term outcomes and cost-effectiveness need further research. (PMID 40143213)
- Biologics in IgE-mediated food allergy have limited RCT evidence, and more research is needed on treatment duration and long-term outcomes. (PMID 40510735, PMID 37185824)
- Quality of life benefit was observed after completing an OIT protocol, with limited evidence of benefit during active treatment. (PMID 40919791)
- The GA2LEN 2022 guideline addresses approaches to education, prediction of severe reactions, cost-effectiveness of immunotherapy and biologics. (PMID 36119657)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Sustained unresponsiveness after stopping OIT.
- Optimal treatment duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic sequencing in high-risk subgroups of Peanut oral immunotherapy food allergy.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- OIT risks: allergic reactions during build-up dosing.
- Role of omalizumab as adjunct.
- Diagnostic criteria, biomarker thresholds, and risk stratification in Peanut oral immunotherapy food allergy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Teenager with peanut anaphylaxis asking about oral immunotherapy.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Peanut oral immunotherapy food allergy presenting with therapeutic intolerance, refractory symptoms, or acute complication requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Efficacy figures from PALISADE and dose protocols are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources

## [1301] Pediatric ALL immunotherapy and targeted therapy

**Cluster**: paeds_all | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Paediatric B-ALL treatment increasingly integrates blinatumomab, asparaginase optimisation, targeted agents and CAR T-cell therapy for relapsed or refractory disease, with attention to complications such as thrombosis.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ASH 2026 guideline for frontline management of ALL in adolescents and young adults states use of targeted agents in frontline therapy is increasingly supported, though further research is needed. (PMID 41670627)
- Recommendations address integrating frontline blinatumomab, asparaginase and antimetabolites, and asparaginase hypersensitivity and formulation issues. (PMID 42578884)
- A meta-analysis of CAR T-cell therapy in relapsed or refractory B-ALL found promising results with anti-CD22 CAR T, and new costimulatory domains and targets could enhance efficacy. (PMID 38738799)
- A practice guideline gives GRADE-based recommendations for preparing children and young adults with B-ALL for CAR T-cell therapy, and an international consensus defines B-cell recovery after CD19 CAR T. (PMID 38488312, PMID 41367923)
- Anticoagulant treatment of venous thromboembolism in children with ALL should be tailored to thrombosis site, clinical and radiological evolution and ongoing asparaginase exposure. (PMID 42749602)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Sequencing of immunotherapy and CAR T.
- Optimal treatment duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic sequencing in high-risk subgroups of Pediatric ALL immunotherapy and targeted therapy.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Asparaginase toxicities: pancreatitis, thrombosis, hypersensitivity (not in abstracts).
- CRS and neurotoxicity with CAR T.
- Diagnostic criteria, biomarker thresholds, and risk stratification in Pediatric ALL immunotherapy and targeted therapy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Child with B-ALL relapsing 14 months after end of treatment.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Pediatric ALL immunotherapy and targeted therapy presenting with therapeutic intolerance, refractory symptoms, or acute complication requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Risk stratification, MRD-guided therapy and standard backbone regimens are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

## [1302] PEEP optimisation: recruitment manoeuvres and driving pressure

**Cluster**: peep | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
In ARDS, PEEP is set individually within protective ventilation, with guidelines advising against routine muscle relaxants or corticosteroids in moderate to severe disease. Electrical impedance tomography is a promising but unproven titration tool.

### Core Teaching Points
- A 2026 German guideline advises keeping respiratory parameters in the protective range, adjusting PEEP individually, and not routinely using muscle relaxation or corticosteroids in moderate to severe ARDS. (PMID 41495012)
- The ATS 2024 update provides recommendations for adult ARDS and stresses individual patient factors while further trials are awaited. (PMID 38032683)
- Electrical impedance tomography-guided PEEP titration has potential to prevent ventilator-induced lung injury, but larger studies are needed. (PMID 38512400)
- During surgery, high PEEP with recruitment manoeuvres had a high posterior probability of benefit on postoperative pulmonary complications versus low PEEP, particularly in laparoscopy. (PMID 39042027)
- Perioperative guidelines promote protective ventilation strategies with standardised definitions to reduce variability. (PMID 42612906)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether driving-pressure-guided PEEP improves mortality.
- Optimal treatment duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic sequencing in high-risk subgroups of PEEP optimisation: recruitment manoeuvres and driving pressure.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Low tidal volume and plateau pressure limits in ARDS.
- Recruitment manoeuvres harms (not in abstracts).
- Diagnostic criteria, biomarker thresholds, and risk stratification in PEEP optimisation: recruitment manoeuvres and driving pressure.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Moderate ARDS with P/F 120 and plateau pressure 31.
- A complex multimorbid patient with PEEP optimisation: recruitment manoeuvres and driving pressure presenting with therapeutic intolerance, refractory symptoms, or acute complication requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Driving pressure targets and PEEP tables, and evidence from ART and ALVEOLI trials, are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38032683**: *An Update on Management of Adult Patients with Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome: An Official American Thoracic Society Clinical Practice Guideline* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 38512400**: *Electrical impedance tomography-guided positive end-expiratory pressure titration in ARDS: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 39042027**: *High Positive End-expiratory Pressure (PEEP) with Recruitment Maneuvers versus Low PEEP during General Anesthesia for Surgery: A Bayesian Individual Patient Data Meta-analysis of Three Randomized Clinical Trials* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None

## [1303] Pegloticase and lesinurad in refractory gout: indications, infusion reactions

**Cluster**: pegloticase | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Pegloticase is considered for refractory gout that fails conventional urate-lowering therapy, and lesinurad as an add-on uricosuric. Flare prophylaxis is advised during the first months of urate lowering.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2016 EULAR recommendations advise flare prophylaxis with low-dose colchicine or NSAIDs during the first 6 months of urate-lowering therapy and that pegloticase can be considered in refractory gout. (PMID 28430170, PMID 27457514)
- An international position paper defined the place of the uricosuric lesinurad when it was introduced. (PMID 30244431)
- A review of gout treatments highlights emerging targeted and biologic therapies that may help severe or refractory disease. (PMID 28658988)
- Cardiovascular effects of urate-lowering therapies in chronic gout showed no clear differences, but trials were short and had few events. (PMID 28379501)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Cardiovascular safety comparisons among urate-lowering drugs.
- Optimal management duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic de-escalation in high-risk subgroups of Pegloticase and lesinurad in refractory gout: indications, infusion reactions.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Pegloticase infusion reactions and anti-drug antibodies (not in abstracts).
- G6PD testing before pegloticase.
- Recognizing treatment refractoriness, adverse drug reaction monitoring, and urgent escalation pathways in Pegloticase and lesinurad in refractory gout: indications, infusion reactions.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Tophaceous gout with serum urate 600 despite maximal allopurinol and febuxostat.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Pegloticase and lesinurad in refractory gout: indications, infusion reactions presenting with therapeutic intolerance, contraindications, or disease escalation requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Pegloticase dosing, infusion reaction prevention and methotrexate co-therapy are not stated in the retrieved abstracts; lesinurad has since been withdrawn.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27457514**: *2016 updated EULAR evidence-based recommendations for the management of gout* (2017) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 28379501**: *Cardiovascular effects of urate-lowering therapies in patients with chronic gout: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2017) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 28430170**: *Current management of gout: practical messages from 2016 EULAR guidelines* (2017) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 28658988**: *Updates on the treatment of gout, including a review of updated treatment guidelines and use of small molecule therapies for difficult-to-treat gout and gout flares* (2017) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 30244431**: *International position paper on the appropriate use of uricosurics with the introduction of lesinurad* (2018) [guideline] - None

## [1304] pelvic inflammatory disease

**Cluster**: pid | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Pelvic inflammatory disease is treated with broad-spectrum antibiotics covering gonorrhoea, chlamydia, anaerobes and increasingly Mycoplasma genitalium. European and French guidelines differ in first-line regimens.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2017 European guideline recommends testing for Mycoplasma genitalium in women with possible PID and male partners of confirmed cases, now recommends moxifloxacin as first-line (notably with M genitalium PID), suggests doxycycline for partners to limit macrolide exposure, and highlights MRI to exclude differential diagnoses. (PMID 29198181)
- French guidelines select ceftriaxone-doxycycline-metronidazole as first-line for uncomplicated PID, with moxifloxacin or levofloxacin/ofloxacin plus metronidazole as alternatives, and moxifloxacin for detected M genitalium. (PMID 30878689)
- A Cochrane review found azithromycin probably improves cure rates in mild-moderate PID compared with doxycycline (RR 1.35), and little or no difference between quinolones and cephalosporins for cure. (PMID 32820536)
- Empirical antimicrobial treatment for pelvic inflammatory disease (PID) must be initiated promptly in sexually active women with lower abdominal pain and cervical motion, uterine, or adnexal tenderness to prevent tubal scarring, ectopic pregnancy, and infertility; recommended regimens include intramuscular ceftriaxone (500 mg) plus oral doxycycline (100 mg twice daily) and oral metronidazole (400-500 mg twice daily) for 14 days. (PMID 29198181)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal regimen as M genitalium macrolide resistance rises.
- Diagnostic sensitivity of transvaginal ultrasound versus endometrial biopsy in subclinical or atypical pelvic inflammatory disease.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome (not in abstracts).
- Treat partners and screen for gonorrhoea, chlamydia and M genitalium.
- Diagnostic criteria (cervical motion tenderness) and empirical dual-antibiotic outpatient regimens (ceftriaxone, doxycycline, metronidazole) for PID.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Young woman with lower abdominal pain, cervical motion tenderness and fever.
- A 22-year-old female presenting with bilateral lower pelvic pain, deep dyspareunia, and purulent cervical discharge with marked cervical excitation on bimanual examination.

**Reviewer Gap**: Diagnostic minimum criteria, admission criteria, IV regimens and follow-up are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29198181**: *2017 European guideline for the management of pelvic inflammatory disease* (2018) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 30878689**: *[Treatment of uncomplicated pelvic inflammatory disease: CNGOF and SPILF Pelvic Inflammatory Diseases Guidelines]* (2019) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 32820536**: *Antibiotic therapy for pelvic inflammatory disease* (2020) [meta-analysis] - None

## [1305] Pelvic inflammatory disease: diagnosis and treatment

**Cluster**: pid | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Pelvic inflammatory disease is treated with broad-spectrum antibiotics covering gonorrhoea, chlamydia, anaerobes and increasingly Mycoplasma genitalium. European and French guidelines differ in first-line regimens.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2017 European guideline recommends testing for Mycoplasma genitalium in women with possible PID and male partners of confirmed cases, now recommends moxifloxacin as first-line (notably with M genitalium PID), suggests doxycycline for partners to limit macrolide exposure, and highlights MRI to exclude differential diagnoses. (PMID 29198181)
- French guidelines select ceftriaxone-doxycycline-metronidazole as first-line for uncomplicated PID, with moxifloxacin or levofloxacin/ofloxacin plus metronidazole as alternatives, and moxifloxacin for detected M genitalium. (PMID 30878689)
- A Cochrane review found azithromycin probably improves cure rates in mild-moderate PID compared with doxycycline (RR 1.35), and little or no difference between quinolones and cephalosporins for cure. (PMID 32820536)
- Inpatient admission and parenteral antibiotic therapy (intravenous cefotetan/cefoxitin plus doxycycline, or clindamycin plus gentamicin) are indicated for PID complicated by pregnancy, tubo-ovarian abscess, inability to tolerate oral medications, lack of response to outpatient therapy after 72 hours, or diagnostic uncertainty (suspected appendicitis). (PMID 29198181)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal regimen as M genitalium macrolide resistance rises.
- Optimal duration of intravenous antimicrobial therapy before conversion to oral regimens in tubo-ovarian abscesses managed without percutaneous drainage.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome (not in abstracts).
- Treat partners and screen for gonorrhoea, chlamydia and M genitalium.
- Inpatient admission indications and parenteral antimicrobial regimens for complicated pelvic inflammatory disease and tubo-ovarian abscesses.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Young woman with lower abdominal pain, cervical motion tenderness and fever.
- A 25-year-old woman with severe bilateral pelvic peritonitis, fever 38.9°C, leukocytosis, and pelvic ultrasound demonstrating a 5 cm complex cystic adnexal mass (tubo-ovarian abscess).

**Reviewer Gap**: Diagnostic minimum criteria, admission criteria, IV regimens and follow-up are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29198181**: *2017 European guideline for the management of pelvic inflammatory disease* (2018) [guideline] - None

## [1306] pembrolizumab plus chemotherapy metastatic non-small cell lung cancer

**Cluster**: nsclc_pembrolizumab | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Adding pembrolizumab to platinum-pemetrexed chemotherapy improved overall and progression-free survival in untreated metastatic nonsquamous NSCLC without EGFR or ALK alterations (KEYNOTE-189), and combination therapy is favoured when rapid response is needed.

### Core Teaching Points
- KEYNOTE-189: in untreated metastatic nonsquamous NSCLC without EGFR or ALK mutations, adding pembrolizumab to pemetrexed and platinum gave estimated 12-month overall survival 69.2% versus 49.4% (HR for death 0.49). (PMID 29658856)
- A meta-analysis found pembrolizumab plus chemotherapy offered greater long-term overall survival than pembrolizumab alone in patients with PD-L1 expression of 1% to 49%. (PMID 39072325)
- A network meta-analysis suggests combination therapy can be considered when rapid response or prevention of rapid progression is needed. (PMID 35341985)
- Pembrolizumab plus chemotherapy was reported as possibly the optimal strategy for major advanced NSCLC with reliable safety, especially in nonsquamous disease. (PMID 34907661)
- Nivolumab plus ipilimumab showed trends toward long-term benefit with manageable toxicity compared with other immunotherapy combinations, and the best combination varies with PD-L1 level. (PMID 36669321, PMID 36389713)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Best option at low PD-L1 expression.
- Optimal management duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic de-escalation in high-risk subgroups of pembrolizumab plus chemotherapy metastatic non-small cell lung cancer.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Check EGFR, ALK and PD-L1 before first-line therapy.
- Immune-related adverse events.
- Recognizing treatment refractoriness, adverse drug reaction monitoring, and urgent escalation pathways in pembrolizumab plus chemotherapy metastatic non-small cell lung cancer.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Metastatic lung adenocarcinoma, PD-L1 20%, no driver mutation: first-line choice.
- A complex multimorbid patient with pembrolizumab plus chemotherapy metastatic non-small cell lung cancer presenting with therapeutic intolerance, contraindications, or disease escalation requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Squamous histology data (KEYNOTE-407), PD-L1 TPS of at least 50% monotherapy and toxicity management are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29658856**: *Pembrolizumab plus Chemotherapy in Metastatic Non-Small-Cell Lung Cancer* (2018) [other] - None
- **PMID 34907661**: *Efficacy and safety of anti-PD-1/PD-L1 in combination with chemotherapy or not as first-line treatment for advanced non-small cell lung cancer: A systematic review and network meta-analysis* (2022) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 35341985**: *Pembrolizumab alone or with chemotherapy for metastatic non-small-cell lung cancer: A systematic review and network meta-analysis* (2022) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 36389713**: *A systematic review and network meta-analysis of first-line immune checkpoint inhibitor combination therapies in patients with advanced non-squamous non-small cell lung cancer* (2022) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 36669321**: *Long-term comparative efficacy and safety of nivolumab plus ipilimumab relative to other first-line therapies for advanced non-small-cell lung cancer: A systematic literature review and network meta-analysis* (2023) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 39072325**: *Comparative long-term outcomes of pembrolizumab plus chemotherapy versus pembrolizumab monotherapy as first-line therapy for metastatic non-small-cell lung cancer: a systematic review and network meta-analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis] - None

## [1307] Penicillin allergy delabeling and testing

**Cluster**: penicillin_allergy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Most patients labelled penicillin-allergic are not allergic. Delabelling by direct oral challenge or structured testing is safe, can be done by non-allergists, and improves antibiotic choice.

### Core Teaching Points
- Penicillin allergy delabelling by non-allergists is efficacious and safe, with reported harm about 0%; the proportion delabelled rises with complexity of testing, but many can be delabelled without skin testing. (PMID 36450321)
- Reactions to direct penicillin challenges are infrequent and comparable to indirect challenges after allergy testing, so direct challenge is safe across age groups and settings. (PMID 39283610)
- BSACI guidance supports delabelling services run by non-allergists for low-risk patients, with a framework for drug provocation testing and separate adult and paediatric sections. (PMID 36128691)
- Hong Kong consensus supports testing by non-allergists, with low-risk cases managed at spoke clinics supported by an allergist-led hub, and removal of inaccurate labels after a negative provocation test with counselling. (PMID 36133403)
- Surgical patients with a penicillin allergy label can be assessed and tested by trained professionals meeting minimum safe-testing standards. (PMID 30916014)
- The reported global prevalence of penicillin allergy comes mainly from high-income countries and secondary care. (PMID 39874990)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Data from low-income settings.
- Optimal management duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic de-escalation in high-risk subgroups of Penicillin allergy delabeling and testing.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Direct oral amoxicillin challenge for low-risk histories.
- Why delabelling improves antimicrobial stewardship.
- Recognizing treatment refractoriness, adverse drug reaction monitoring, and urgent escalation pathways in Penicillin allergy delabeling and testing.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient with childhood rash on amoxicillin needing perioperative cefazolin.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Penicillin allergy delabeling and testing presenting with therapeutic intolerance, contraindications, or disease escalation requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Risk-stratification tools (PEN-FAST), management of severe delayed reactions and cephalosporin cross-reactivity are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30916014**: *Management of a surgical patient with a label of penicillin allergy: narrative review and consensus recommendations* (2019) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 36128691**: *BSACI guideline for the set-up of penicillin allergy de-labelling services by non-allergists working in a hospital setting* (2022) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 36133403**: *Hong Kong Drug Allergy Delabelling Initiative (HK-DADI) consensus statements for penicillin allergy testing by nonallergists* (2022) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 36450321**: *The effectiveness of interventions that support penicillin allergy assessment and delabeling of adult and pediatric patients by nonallergy specialists: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2023) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 39283610**: *Reaction Risk to Direct Penicillin Challenges: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 39874990**: *The global prevalence of reported penicillin allergy: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None

## [1309] Peptic ulcer bleeding and H pylori

**Cluster**: peptic_ulcer | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Peptic ulcer disease management centres on H. pylori eradication guided by local resistance, acid suppression, endoscopic haemostasis of bleeding ulcers and PPI protection in patients needing antiplatelets.

### Core Teaching Points
- A scoping review of H. pylori guidelines stresses that health authorities must update guidance in step with surveillance of local resistance patterns. (PMID 40346683)
- The ACG 2024 clinical practice guideline addresses treatment of H. pylori infection including salvage therapy and the role of susceptibility testing. (PMID 39626064)
- Vonoprazan-based triple therapy achieved high eradication rates in dual-resistant H. pylori and is a viable salvage option; an Egyptian consensus validates high-dose dual therapy. (PMID 41138484, PMID 42412729)
- Guidelines for endoscopic management of non-variceal upper GI bleeding (second edition) pool new evidence, though levels of evidence and recommendations remain mostly low. (PMID 40114631)
- For patients with prior ulcer bleeding who need antiplatelet or anticoagulant therapy for cardiovascular prophylaxis, an international consensus suggests PPI therapy. (PMID 31634917)
- Japanese guidelines recommend PPIs or vonoprazan to prevent ulcer recurrence in patients with a history of ulcer on low-dose aspirin. (PMID 33620586)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal third-line regimens.
- Optimal management duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic de-escalation in high-risk subgroups of Peptic ulcer bleeding and H pylori.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Test and treat H pylori; confirm eradication.
- Endoscopic therapy for high-risk stigmata then high-dose PPI (not in abstracts).
- Recognizing treatment refractoriness, adverse drug reaction monitoring, and urgent escalation pathways in Peptic ulcer bleeding and H pylori.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient on aspirin with melaena and a Forrest Ib duodenal ulcer.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Peptic ulcer bleeding and H pylori presenting with therapeutic intolerance, contraindications, or disease escalation requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: First-line regimen composition, Forrest classification and rebleeding prevention are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31634917**: *Management of Nonvariceal Upper Gastrointestinal Bleeding: Guideline Recommendations From the International Consensus Group* (2019) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 33620586**: *Evidence-based clinical practice guidelines for peptic ulcer disease 2020* (2021) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 39626064**: *ACG Clinical Guideline: Treatment of Helicobacter pylori Infection* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 40114631**: *Guidelines for endoscopic management of nonvariceal upper gastrointestinal bleeding (second edition)* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 40346683**: *A scoping review of worldwide guidelines for diagnosis and treatment of Helicobacter pylori infection* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 41138484**: *Vonoprazan-based triple therapy for Helicobacter pylori resistant to first-line regimens: A guideline-compliant 14-day multicenter study in Egypt* (2026) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 42412729**: *Consensus Recommendations for the Management of &lt;i&gt;Helicobacter pylori&lt;/i&gt; in Egypt: A 2026 Report* (2026) [guideline] - None

## [1311] Perinatal depression screening and treatment

**Cluster**: perinatal_depression | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Perinatal depression screening is under-implemented compared with guideline recommendations, and treatment options now include neurosteroids (brexanolone, zuranolone), updated pharmacotherapy guidance, exercise and psychological interventions.

### Core Teaching Points
- A systematic review found a significant gap between recommendations for perinatal depression screening and their implementation, highlighting the need to integrate routine screening and referral into maternal care. (PMID 38733723)
- ACOG clinical guidance covers zuranolone and brexanolone for postpartum depression and replaces earlier practice advisory on zuranolone. (PMID 41066774)
- Updated guidance on pharmacologic treatment of perinatal depression summarises recommendations for nonpsychiatric clinicians. (PMID 41922074)
- A network meta-analysis supports stage-specific, volume-adjusted exercise prescriptions, with mind-body exercise more efficacious postpartum. (PMID 41531421)
- Antenatal depression increases the risk of postpartum depression, with about 12.8% of postpartum depression cases attributable to it, informing screening frequency and follow-up. (PMID 37742599)
- Non-pharmacological interventions delivered by trained health professionals may treat perinatal depression among adolescent mothers. (PMID 40081580)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal screening interval.
- Optimal management duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic de-escalation in high-risk subgroups of Perinatal depression screening and treatment.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Screen with EPDS or PHQ-9 in pregnancy and postpartum.
- Antidepressant choice in breastfeeding (not in abstracts).
- Recognizing treatment refractoriness, adverse drug reaction monitoring, and urgent escalation pathways in Perinatal depression screening and treatment.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Woman 3 weeks postpartum with low mood and intrusive thoughts.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Perinatal depression screening and treatment presenting with therapeutic intolerance, contraindications, or disease escalation requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Screening tools and thresholds, SSRI safety data and suicide risk assessment are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37742599**: *Antenatal depression across trimesters as a risk for postpartum depression and estimation of the fraction of postpartum depression attributable to antenatal depression: A systematic review and meta-analysis of cohort studies* (2023) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 38733723**: *Gaps between current practice in perinatal depression screening and guideline recommendations: a systematic review* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 40081580**: *Non-pharmacological interventions for perinatal depression and anxiety among adolescent mothers: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None

## [1312] Periodic paralyses: hypokaemic vs hyperkaemic variants, channelopathies

**Cluster**: periodic_paralysis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Periodic paralysis includes hypokalaemic and hyperkalaemic channelopathies and thyrotoxic forms. Treatment is trigger avoidance, potassium modification, diuretics and carbonic anhydrase inhibitors, with dichlorphenamide the best-supported drug.

### Core Teaching Points
- A meta-analysis supports dichlorphenamide in periodic paralysis with high-certainty evidence for primary outcomes and consistent efficacy across aetiologies and ages, although side effects need monitoring and counselling. (PMID 41371188)
- Treatment of periodic paralysis includes behavioural avoidance of triggers, modification of potassium levels, diuretics and carbonic anhydrase inhibitors. (PMID 29125635)
- A systematic review of hypokalaemic periodic paralysis case reports found phenotypic differences linked to different mutations and a suboptimal response to common preventive treatment. (PMID 33085076)
- A genome-wide meta-analysis found susceptibility loci for thyrotoxic periodic paralysis, including variants near genes implicated in monogenic muscle paralysis (KCNJ2, TRIM2). (PMID 33105104)
- Mutation-specific alterations in NaV1.4 function explain the diverse phenotypes of skeletal muscle sodium channelopathies. (PMID 28939973)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Best preventive drug by genotype.
- Optimal treatment duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic sequencing in high-risk subgroups of Periodic paralyses: hypokaemic vs hyperkaemic variants, channelopathies.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Low potassium during attacks in hypokalaemic periodic paralysis; careful potassium replacement.
- Thyrotoxic periodic paralysis in Asian men.
- Diagnostic criteria, biomarker thresholds, and risk stratification in Periodic paralyses: hypokaemic vs hyperkaemic variants, channelopathies.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Young man with morning flaccid weakness after a carbohydrate-rich meal and low potassium.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Periodic paralyses: hypokaemic vs hyperkaemic variants, channelopathies presenting with therapeutic intolerance, refractory symptoms, or acute complication requiring multidisciplinary management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Attack treatment, precipitants and the hyperkalaemic variant's treatment details are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28939973**: *Sodium Channelopathies of Skeletal Muscle* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 29125635**: *Review of the Diagnosis and Treatment of Periodic Paralysis* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 33085076**: *[Hypokalemic periodic paralysis: a systematic review of published case reports]* (2020) [systematic review] - None
- **PMID 33105104**: *Genome-wide meta-analysis reveals novel susceptibility loci for thyrotoxic periodic paralysis* (2020) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 41371188**: *Safety and efficacy of dichlorphenamide in patients with periodic paralysis: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2026) [meta-analysis] - None

## [1313] Perioperative antiplatelet and anticoagulant management

**Cluster**: periop_antithrombotic | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Perioperative antithrombotic management balances thrombosis against bleeding. Evidence is limited for most questions; DOACs can be interrupted and resumed without heparin bridging, and procedure-specific guidance exists for interventional pain, emergency and gastrostomy procedures.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ACCP perioperative antithrombotic guideline notes uncertainty remains about best practice for most perioperative management questions. (PMID 35964704)
- The 2025 DOAC guidance states DOACs can be interrupted and resumed for procedures without bridging with LMWH. (PMID 40448969)
- A 2024 consensus on interventional pain procedures reached 100% acceptance on 16 of 18 final items for managing antiplatelet and anticoagulant therapy, with disagreement on two statements. (PMID 39133736)
- French emergency medicine recommendations provide up-to-date management of patients on anticoagulants in emergency settings with strong expert agreement. (PMID 40581261)
- The ASGE gastrostomy guideline calls for multidisciplinary discussion of bleeding versus cardiovascular risk in patients on antithrombotics. (PMID 39520459)
- Canadian radiology practice guidelines address periprocedural management of bleeding risk, aiming to avoid unnecessary delays and consultations. (PMID 41964243)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Bridging is rarely needed but data are limited.
- Optimal management duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic de-escalation in high-risk subgroups of Perioperative antiplatelet and anticoagulant management.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- No bridging for most DOAC interruptions.
- Continue aspirin for secondary prevention in most surgery (not in abstracts).
- Recognizing treatment refractoriness, adverse drug reaction monitoring, and urgent escalation pathways in Perioperative antiplatelet and anticoagulant management.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient on apixaban for AF scheduled for colectomy: interruption plan.
- A complex multimorbid patient with Perioperative antiplatelet and anticoagulant management presenting with therapeutic intolerance, contraindications, or disease escalation requiring multidisciplinary management.

## [1314] perioperative beta-blocker continuation versus withdrawal

**Cluster**: periop_betablocker | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Starting beta-blockers just before non-cardiac surgery increases harm: in POISE metoprolol cut myocardial infarction but increased death and stroke. Established beta-blocker therapy is generally continued rather than withdrawn.

### Core Teaching Points
- POISE: extended-release metoprolol started before non-cardiac surgery reduced a cardiac outcome (HR 0.73) but increased deaths (3.1% vs 2.3%) and strokes (1.0% vs 0.5%). (PMID 18479744)
- Beta-blockers do not improve perioperative outcomes in vascular and endovascular surgery in a meta-analysis. (PMID 28039238)
- After cardiac surgery, perioperative beta-blockers prevent postoperative atrial fibrillation but did not translate into improved clinical outcomes. (PMID 32252112)
- In CABG, nearly all patients without contraindications are candidates for perioperative beta-blocker therapy, with carvedilol the most beneficial in one review. (PMID 30037300)
- Observational data suggest beta-blocker exposure relates to 30-day all-cause mortality but not to 30-day MACE or stroke, and commentary highlights potential harm of perioperative withdrawal. (PMID 32169568, PMID 29935568)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal handling in patients with established beta-blockade.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Do not start high-dose beta-blocker on the day of surgery.
- Continue chronic beta-blockers perioperatively.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient on bisoprolol for 5 years scheduled for hip replacement.

**Reviewer Gap**: Guideline class recommendations (continue versus initiate in high-risk patients) are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 18479744**: *Effects of extended-release metoprolol succinate in patients undergoing non-cardiac surgery (POISE trial): a randomised controlled trial* (2008) [other] - None
- **PMID 28039238**: *Effect of beta-blockers on perioperative outcomes in vascular and endovascular surgery: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2017) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 29935568**: *Beta-blockers in noncardiac surgery: Did observational studies put us back on safe ground?* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 30037300**: *Rationale for administering beta-blocker therapy to patients undergoing coronary artery bypass surgery: a systematic review* (2018) [systematic review] - None
- **PMID 32169568**: *Beta-blocker exposure for short-term outcomes following non-cardiac surgery: A meta-analysis of observational studies* (2020) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 32252112**: *Perioperative Beta-Blocker for Atrial Fibrillation after Cardiac Surgery: A Meta-Analysis* (2021) [meta-analysis] - None

## [1315] Perioperative bridging anticoagulation

**Cluster**: periop_antithrombotic | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Perioperative antithrombotic management balances thrombosis against bleeding. Evidence is limited for most questions; DOACs can be interrupted and resumed without heparin bridging, and procedure-specific guidance exists for interventional pain, emergency and gastrostomy procedures.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ACCP perioperative antithrombotic guideline notes uncertainty remains about best practice for most perioperative management questions. (PMID 35964704)
- The 2025 DOAC guidance states DOACs can be interrupted and resumed for procedures without bridging with LMWH. (PMID 40448969)
- A 2024 consensus on interventional pain procedures reached 100% acceptance on 16 of 18 final items for managing antiplatelet and anticoagulant therapy, with disagreement on two statements. (PMID 39133736)
- French emergency medicine recommendations provide up-to-date management of patients on anticoagulants in emergency settings with strong expert agreement. (PMID 40581261)
- The ASGE gastrostomy guideline calls for multidisciplinary discussion of bleeding versus cardiovascular risk in patients on antithrombotics. (PMID 39520459)
- Canadian radiology practice guidelines address periprocedural management of bleeding risk, aiming to avoid unnecessary delays and consultations. (PMID 41964243)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Bridging is rarely needed but data are limited.
- Optimal management duration, biomarker monitoring thresholds, and personalized therapeutic de-escalation in high-risk subgroups of Perioperative bridging anticoagulation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- No bridging for most DOAC interruptions.
- Continue aspirin for secondary prevention in most surgery (not in abstracts).
- Recognizing treatment refractoriness, adverse drug reaction monitoring, and urgent escalation pathways in Perioperative bridging anticoagulation.
